III. 
SAP TRANSITION AND CUSTOMER SERVICE PERFORMANCE 
7 
Q.    
Please discuss the SAP CIS transition. 
8 
A. 
On September 1, 2021, Central Hudson launched its new SAP-based CIS.  
9 
Immediately after the launch, there were issues with its new SAP CIS that 
10 
resulted in impacts to a portion of Central Hudson’s customers, including 
11 
through delayed and inaccurate invoices, confusion, and anxiety.  Despite 
12 
Central Hudson’s best efforts to correct the issues, the issues continued, 
13 
and Central Hudson experienced a rise in customer complaint levels, 
14 
harsh criticism from politicians, and questions from the Commission and 
15 
others seeking to understand the problems with the SAP CIS.  Central 
16 
Hudson redoubled its efforts with the help of its union and management 
17 
employees, third-party experts, and contractors to correct all issues. 
18 
Q.    
Has Central Hudson tracked the impact to service levels associated with 
19 
the launch of SAP CIS and associated events? 
20 
A. 
Yes, the Company developed a dashboard which outlines Central 
21 
Hudson’s progress along six key metrics.  These metrics are % Current 
22 
Bills, % Bills Requiring Adjustments, Actual Bills Replaced with Estimates, 
23 
2994

Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____

1 
the date of this filing, the most recent version of this dashboard dated July 
2 
20, 2023, has been included as Exhibit __ (CEP-1). 
3

8 
SAP CIS issues.

A. 
IMPLEMENTATION OF MONTHLY METER READING 
8 
Q.    
Please briefly describe the Company’s proposed transition to monthly 
9 
meter reading from the present bi-monthly structure.   
10 
A. 
Central Hudson has evaluated and developed a plan to adjust its billing 
11 
practices and to conduct monthly meter reads, thereby eliminating 
12 
alternate month bill estimates.  This plan was filed on January 17, 2023, in 
13 
Case 22-M-0645 and fully lays out the timeline and the incremental costs 
14 
and resources required for the transition.  On July 27, 2023, DPS Staff 
15 
and the Company reached an interim agreement which prescribes an 
16 
acceleration of the originally proposed timeline. The transition to monthly 
17 
meter reading is aligned with residential customer sentiments that show a 
18 
preference for monthly meter reads and dissatisfaction with bi-monthly 
19 
billing estimates.  Due to the timing of the interim agreement, this item is 
20 
subject to update as stated in the testimony of the Revenue Requirements 
21 
Panel.  
22 
2998


--- Page 30 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE CUSTOMER EXPERIENCE PANEL 
14 
 
Q.    
Why does the Monthly Meter Reading Plan propose a phased roll out? 
1 
A. 
A phased roll out of monthly meter reading is designed to protect 
2 
customers by limiting any programming defects discovered within the “real 
3 
world environment” to the smallest group of customers possible.  The 
4 
intent of the phased roll-out would be to first implement the program for a 
5 
very specific subset of customers, then to expand the program to an initial 
6 
meter reading route, and next to each of the Company’s five districts, until 
7 
full scale implementation is achieved.   
8 
Q.    
Please describe the incremental annual costs and resources required to 
9 
implement monthly meter reading. 
10 
A. 
The incremental annual costs equate to approximately $4.4 million.  These 
11 
incremental costs are comprised of recuring and one-time operating and 
12 
maintenance (“O&M”) costs as well the return on and of approximately 
13 
$1.8 million of capital expenditures.    
14 
 Annual O&M costs related to the transition to monthly meter reading 
15 
will be approximately $4.0 million.  The majority of this cost is 
16 
attributable to additional meter reading resources, with the small 
17 
remainder driven by maintenance costs of meter reading hardware 
18 
and maintenance and fuel costs for meter reading vehicles.  Meter 
19 
reading resources are assumed to be fulfilled by both external 
20 
2999


--- Page 31 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE CUSTOMER EXPERIENCE PANEL 
15 
 
contractors and internal labor.  Based on the timeline noted above, 
1 
these resources will be hired subsequent to the Rate Year.    
2 
 Capital Expenditures related to the transition to monthly meter reading 
3 
are estimated to be approximately $1.8 million, largely driven by 
4 
vehicle purchases and technology investments such as meter reader 
5 
equipment, mobile radios, tough-pad tablets, and programming 
6 
modifications in the SAP CIS system.  These costs are included within 
7 
the Company’s capital forecast.  Additionally, costs associated with 
8 
the return on and of associated capital investments are estimated to 
9 
be approximately $0.4 million. 
10 
 In addition to the recurring costs noted above, one-time O&M expense 
11 
associated with a customer outreach campaign is estimated to cost 
12 
$0.2 million. 
13 
Q.    
Does the Company have anything else to add regarding the transition to 
14 
monthly meter reading? 
15 
A. 
Given the timing of the interim agreement, the full costs associated with 
16 
the revised monthly meter reading plan included in the interim agreement 
17 
are not reflected in the Rate Year revenue requirements.  As such, the 
18 
Company proposes that revenue requirements be updated at a later stage 
19 
in this proceeding for all impacted components of revenue requirements 
20 
pertaining to the monthly meter reading plan.

B. 
IMPROVEMENT OF CUSTOMER EXPERIENCE TECHNOLOGY  
1 
Q.    
Please describe the Company’s technology investments that will improve 
2 
communications and interactions with customers while providing more 
3 
self-service options and increasing the ease of use for key existing self-
4 
service options. 
5 
A. 
The Company seeks to implement a portfolio of projects in the Rate Year 
6 
ending June 30, 2025, requiring $5.3 million in capital expenditures.  
7 
These projects are also reflected in Exhibit __ (CEP-2) and discussed in 
8 
testimony of the Technology Capital and Operations Panel.  The portfolio 
9 
contains the following projects: 1) Interactive Voice Response (“IVR”) 
10 
Modernization, 2) Customer Web and Mobile Technology Improvements 
11 
including a dedicated experience for commercial customers, and 3) a 
12 
centralized notification system.  
13 
Q.    
What are the current limitations and challenges of the Company’s current 
14 
IVR system that require modernization? 
15 
A. 
A major limitation of the current IVR system is the ability to integrate with 
16 
other channels.  Many customers interact with the Company via a mobile 
17 
phone to call, visit the website, or navigate the mobile application.  
18 
However, the current IVR system was designed prior to increased 
19 
customer demand for that customer journey and does not include 
20 
integration with web or application-based service offerings.  Additionally, 
21 
the current IVR solution is limited in its ability to easily make efficiency 
22 
changes to the routing and handling of calls during high-volume or storm 
23 
3001


--- Page 33 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE CUSTOMER EXPERIENCE PANEL 
17 
 
situations.  Finally, the Company needs the ability to make changes 
1 
without relying on a vendor, which creates long lead-times, as is done 
2 
today.  
3 
Q.    
Please describe the Company’s investment in IVR modernization. 
4 
A. 
Central Hudson will invest approximately $1.7 million within the Rate Year 
5 
and $3 million by the end of 2025 to replace the current on-premises IVR 
6 
system with a cloud-based IVR solution that will enable features such as 
7 
Voice Recognition, and VoiceBots that will augment contact center live 
8 
agent support by providing self-service voice capabilities for customers.  
9 
Additionally, features such as Visual IVR and predictive call routing will be 
10 
implemented, to match the call with the optimal Contact Center agents 
11 
that have been provided additional information and analytics on the 
12 
customer call.  Finally, the new IVR will allow customers on digital 
13 
platforms to seamlessly transition between channels. 
14 
Q.    
What enhancements to Customer Web and Mobile Technology is the 
15 
Company proposing? 
16 
A. 
Central Hudson is proposing approximately $3.6 million during the Rate 
17 
Year to enhance its customer-facing Web and Mobile Technology 
18 
interfaces.  First, the Company will be evaluating all customer-facing 
19 
digital channels for alignment with the Americans with Disabilities Act 
20 
standards.  This evaluation will identify gaps to allow future enhancements 
21 
to be in alignment with standards for customers with disabilities.  Second, 
22 
the Company will be redesigning the MyAccount page on its website as 
23 
3002


--- Page 34 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE CUSTOMER EXPERIENCE PANEL 
18 
 
well as the mobile application.  Approximately 60% of customers have an 
1 
active online account with Central Hudson.  Efforts are being made to 
2 
capture additional customers through various outreach channels.  These 
3 
customers access their accounts via their MyAccount page for information 
4 
about their bills, make payments, and enroll in a variety of programs.  
5 
Concurrently with bill design efforts, the MyAccount page will become 
6

Q.

Q.    
What changes is the Company proposing associated with the PSC 
1 
Complaint Rate? 
2 
A. 
The Company proposes excluding the complaints associated with 
3 
commodity prices that are outside of the Company’s control. 
4 
Q.    
Why does the Company believe this exclusion is appropriate? 
5 
A. 
Complaints around this topic are outside of the Company’s control.  
6 
Therefore, complaints resulting from the price of electric and/or gas 
7 
energy and/or capacity or the operation of the Company’s Energy Cost 
8 
Adjustment Mechanism (“ECAM”) and/or Gas Supply Charge (“GSC”) do 
9 
not present a just cause for charging a complaint against the Company 
10 
and do not show or point to any deficiency in the Company’s service to 
11 
customers.  Additionally, this exclusion would increase alignment across 
12 
the state, as this exclusion was included in Con Edison’s Joint Proposal 
13 
recently approved by the Commission. 
14 
Q.    
Is the Company proposing a change to the target associated with the PSC 
15 
Complaint Rate metric? 
16 
A. 
No, the Company believes that, with the proposed adjustment to the 
17 
calculation of the metric, no change to the target is needed at this time.

19 
the Residential Customer Satisfaction Survey. 
20

Central Hudson proposes to replace the Residential Customer Satisfaction 
21 
Survey instrument with the statewide customer satisfaction survey 
22 
designed by the utilities and DPS Staff and implemented on a pilot basis 
23

Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____

28 
 
per the October 18, 2018, Order in Case 15-M-0566 (“Survey Order”).  
1 
The Customer Satisfaction Index will be calculated from one question, with 
2 
a five-point scale for customer satisfaction.  The question is “Thinking 
3 
about your most recent transaction with Central Hudson, how satisfied are 
4 
you?” The responses are scored as follows: (1) Very Dissatisfied, (2), 
5 
Dissatisfied, (3) Neither Satisfied or Dissatisfied, (4) Satisfied, (5) Very 
6 
Satisfied.  Finally, Central Hudson proposes that the Residential Customer 
7 
Satisfaction Survey metric be split into two survey groups (High Priority 
8 
interactions and General Account & Billing interactions).  These changes 
9 
will more accurately and appropriately measure customer sentiment and 
10 
better align the Company’s metrics with statewide methodologies.    
11 
Q.    
Please describe why the statewide survey is more appropriate than the 
12 
current Residential Customer Satisfaction Survey. 
13 
A. 
The statewide survey method is a better representation of customer 
14 
satisfaction than the current survey for the following reasons: 1) the survey 
15 
was designed to align with current customer preferences to encourage 
16 
participation through ease of use instead of filling out a cumbersome 
17 
paper-based survey; 2) the survey will include customers who interact with 
18 
the Company through multiple channels including phone, text, web & 
19 
mobile app self-service channels, and also field visits; 3) the survey is a 
20 
consistent measurement that all New York State Utilities utilize and was 
21 
recommended for permanent use by the DPS.

VI. 
THE COVID PANDEMIC AFTERMATH AND CUSTOMER ARREARS 
4 
Q.    
Please describe the current state of customer arrears following the 
5 
COVID-19 Pandemic? 
6 
A. 
Customer arrears balances have grown significantly since the Company 
7 
suspended collections activity for all customers in March of 2020.  The 
8 
suspension of collection activities continued until very recently in light of 
9 
the COVID Pandemic, the Parker-Mosley Act of 2020, the Parker-
10 
Richardson Act of 2021, and collection limitations included within the 
11 
Arrears Relief Programs.  From February 2020 through June 30, 2023, 
12 
residential Arrears Balances greater than 60 days grew by 1,044% to $96 
13 
million while non-residential arrears grew by 1,971% to $29 million.  
14 
Additionally, the number of residential customers with arrears greater than 
15 
60 days has grown by 224% to 66,000, while the number of non-
16 
residential customers with arrears has grown by 401% to 10,000.  
17 
Additionally, residential arrears balances less than 60 days have also 
18 
increased since the COVID-19 Pandemic to $16 million while non-
19 
residential arrears grew to $11 million, which are 106% and 308% 
20 
increases, respectively.  Additionally, although the Company has begun a 
21 
very measured approach to restarting collections activities, it is expected 
22 
that these balances will continue to grow prior to July 1, 2024.   
23 
3017


--- Page 49 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE CUSTOMER EXPERIENCE PANEL 
33 
 
Q.    
In light of these historic arrears balances how has the Company 
1 
forecasted uncollectible expense for inclusion in this rate filing? 
2 
A. 
The suspension and measured restart of collection activities has 
3 
significantly distorted the data that would be necessary to develop an 
4 
accurate forecast of uncollectible expense.  At this point, the Company 
5 
cannot reasonably predict the level of net write-offs that will occur in the 
6 
Rate Year.  While there was an uptick in net write-offs experienced in the 
7 
historic year, this was primarily attributable to writing off arrears balances 
8 
for shuttered businesses and residential customers that vacated their 
9 
residences.  At this time, there is a significant level of uncertainty 
10 
surrounding the level of net write-offs that may be experienced, as well as 
11 
the timing.  As a result of the uncertainty, the Company is proposing that it 
12 
(1) maintain the rate allowance for the delivery portion of uncollectibles 
13 
that was provided for in the 2021 Rate Plan for the rate year ended June 
14 
30, 2024; (2) assume the same uncollectible % for fuel and other 
15 
surcharge components of the bill as established in the 2021 Rate Plan; 
16 
and (3) continue with modification the uncollectible true-up mechanism 
17 
afforded under the 2021 Rate Plan. 
18 
Q.    
Why does the Company anticipate uncertainty of net write-offs in the Rate 
19 
Year? 
20 
A. 
The Company’s measured approach to collection activities will provide 
21 
time for customers to acclimate to collection activities and for the 
22 
Company to learn how to best help the customers that require assistance 
23 
3018


--- Page 50 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE CUSTOMER EXPERIENCE PANEL 
34 
 
by determining eligibility for DPAs, the EAP, HEAP, etc.  Reaching the 
1 
unprecedented number of customers in arrears will take time, and any 
2 
subsequently established DPAs will have a significant impact on timing of 
3 
when arrears may actually get written off. 
4 
Q.    
What are the proposed modifications to the existing true-up mechanism? 
5 
A. 
First, accompanying the uncertainty surrounding collections and bad debt 
6 
net write-offs is an uncertainty about future costs for collection agency 
7 
fees.  As such, the Company proposes that the uncollectible true-up 
8 
mechanism should be extended to capture deferral for collection agency 
9 
fees over or under amounts included in rates.  Second, currently the 
10 
Company’s true-up mechanism requires that net write-offs compared to 
11 
bad debt billed exceed a threshold equivalent to ten basis points in order 
12 
for deferral to be allowed.  For the reasons noted above regarding the 
13 
great uncertainty in the magnitude and timing of net write-offs, the 
14 
Company proposes that the ten basis point threshold be removed from the 
15 
operation of the true-up mechanism.  With removal of the threshold, 
16 
customers and the Company will both be fully protected from future 
17 
uncertainty.  It should also be noted that absent a true-up mechanism for 
18 
uncollectible expense, the rate allowances assumed in the development of 
19 
revenue requirements and the proposed uncollectible factors, which as 
20 
previously noted are all consistent with those established for the Rate 
21 
Year ended June 30, 2024, would have to be adjusted. 
22 
3019


--- Page 51 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE CUSTOMER EXPERIENCE PANEL 
35 
 
Q.    
In light of these historic arrears balances and uncertainty around net write-
1

11

12

16

17

18 
comprised of both full time and part-time employees.  The number of 
19 
residential customers in arrears has increased from a pre-pandemic level 
20 
of approximately 21,000 to a current level of 66,000.  During the pre-
21 
pandemic period Central Hudson handled an annual average of 340,000 
22 
collection-related calls that correspond to the approximately 21,000 
23 
3020


--- Page 52 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE CUSTOMER EXPERIENCE PANEL 
36 
 
residential customers in arrears.  Central Hudson is forecasting the level 
1 
of collection calls to increase at the same proportion as residential 
2 
customers in arrears resulting in approximately 1.1 million collection 
3 
related calls in 2024.  In order to handle the expected significant influx of 
4 
collection calls while also meeting customer service expectations, the 
5 
Company will need to hire 33 incremental customer service FTEs and 
6 
continue an increased use of external call center personnel.  Expense 
7 
associated with the external call center cost is estimated to be 
8 
approximately $2.7 million in the Rate Year; this estimate was provided to 
9 
the Revenue Requirements Panel for inclusion in the development of 
10 
revenue requirements.

Q.    
Has the Company identified the need to create a group to focus directly on 
12 
difficult types of billing?  
13

19

20

Q.    
Please identify the rationale for the incremental Customer Billing 
1 
personnel that will be required. 
2 
A. 
As shown in Exhibit __ (CEP-4), 11 incremental Customer Billing 
3 
personnel will be hired during the bridge period and are required in order 
4 
to investigate an estimated 10% of the collections calls in order to provide 
5 
customers with greater certainty regarding the accuracy of their arrears 
6 
balances.  These customer interactions will be necessary in order to 
7 
provide a positive customer experience and facilitate a positive customer 
8 
action associated with their arrears balance. 
9 
Q.    
Are there other personnel needs that you have identified? 
10 
A. 
Yes.  We will need ten additional field collectors and one additional 
11 
supervisor will also be hired in the bridge period.   
12 
Q.    
Why does Central Hudson require these additional field collectors? 
13 
A. 
Central Hudson’s field collector staffing compliment has traditionally been 
14 
at 11 FTEs, however since the COVID-19 Pandemic and the subsequent 
15 
locking restrictions this compliment currently stands at six.  As discussed 
16 
previously, residential arrears greater than 60 days have increased 
17 
significantly and the Company is increasing the field collector workforce in 
18 
order to facilitate an orderly and measured approach to collections 
19 
activities associated with the unprecedented level of customer arrears. 
20 
3022

5

7

8

9 
continued symmetrical deferral for this element of expense.

A.

III. 
SAP-CIS TRANSITION STATUS 
9 
Q. 
Does the Panel have any initial comments regarding the SAP-Customer 
10 
Information System (“CIS”) transition? 
11 
A. 
Yes, as stated in the direct testimony of the Panel, the Company 
12 
acknowledges that issues with its new SAP-CIS resulted in customer 
13 
confusion and anxiety, a rise in customer complaint levels, harsh criticism 
14 
from elected officials, and questions from the Commission and others 
15 
seeking to understand the problems with the SAP-CIS.  Additionally, as 
16 
stated in the Panel’s direct testimony, the Company has made significant 
17 
progress in remedying all customer billing issues as measured by the % 
18 
Current Bills, the % Bills Requiring Adjustments and Actual Bills Replaced 
19 
with Estimates.  While all billing systems may produce a certain number of 
20 
customer billing issues given the inherent complexity of bill calculation and 
21 
3037


--- Page 69 ---
Case 23-E-0418; Case 23-G-0419 
 
REBUTTAL TESTIMONY OF THE CUSTOMER EXPERIENCE 
PANEL 
11 
 
processing, at this time, customers are receiving accurate and timely bills 
1 
within normal operating parameters. 
2 
Q. 
Does SCSP comment on customer billing issues? 
3 
A. 
Yes, SCSP states that customers are still experiencing issues with the 
4 
Company’s billing and points to the elevated number of customer 
5 
complaints received by the Office of Consumer Services (“OCS”) and the 
6 
comments received at recent Public Statement Hearings expressing 
7 
continued frustration and confusion regarding the Company’s billing. 
8 
Q. 
Does the Panel agree that PSC Complaints received and customer 
9 
comments at Public Statement Hearings are an appropriate barometer for 
10 
continued SAP-CIS billing issues? 
11 
A. 
No, the Panel does not agree that these are appropriate measurements of 
12 
customer billing issues and instead would point back to Exhibit __ (CEP-
13 
2R).  Exhibit __ (CEP-2R) shows the level of % Current Bills, the % Bills 
14 
Requiring Adjustments and Actual Bills Replaced with Estimates, which 
15 
are now within normal operating levels.  Additionally, the Panel notes that 
16 
PSC Complaints received are a lagging indicator and will likely take time 
17 
to return to normal levels as the Company rebuilds customer trust. 
18 
Q. 
Are there other drivers of PSC Complaints received beyond issues related 
19 
to the SAP-CIS transition? 
20 
A. 
Yes, as the Panel will discuss later in its rebuttal testimony, commodity 
21 
prices as well as other factors are related to the number of PSC 
22 
3038


--- Page 70 ---

PANEL 
12 
 
Complaints received and may result in an outsized number of PSC 
1 
Complaints. 
2 
Q. 
Does the Panel have remarks on the use of comments received at Public 
3 
Statement Hearings as a measurement of the existence of continued 
4 
billing issues? 
5 
A. 
Yes, although it is important to understand the tone of customer 
6 
comments and react to customer concerns, Public Statement Hearing 
7 
comments are not an appropriate measure of billing system issues. 
8 
Q. 
Has the Panel performed any analysis of the customer comments 
9 
received at the Public Statement Hearings? 
10 
A. 
Yes, the Panel analyzed the 114 comments received at the in-person 
11 
Public Statement Hearings held in Poughkeepsie, Newburgh, Kingston, 
12 
and Catskill.  Based on the analysis included in Exhibit __ (CEP-3R), out 
13 
of the 114 speakers that provided comments only 7 (or approximately 5%) 
14 
had unresolved issues, primarily open PSC Complaints, at the time they 
15 
provided their comments.  The analysis shows that the majority of billing

17

18

17

18

C. 
PERCENT OF CALLS ANSWERED WITHIN 30 SECONDS 
3 
Q. 
Did SCSP propose any changes to the Company’s proposed target for the 
4 
Percent of Calls Answered within 30 Seconds? 
5 
A. 
No.  However, SCSP did point out a calculation error within the 
6 
Company’s past calculations of the Percent of Calls Answered within 30 
7 
Seconds. 
8 
Q. 
Please describe the calculation error that SCSP identified.  
9 
A.  
SCSP points out that the November 18, 2021 Order Adopting Terms of 
10 
Joint Proposal and Establishing Electric and Gas Rate Plan in Cases 20-
11 
E-0428 and 20-G-0429, defines the calculation of this metric as:  
12 
percentage of calls answered by a Company representative within 30 
13 
seconds of the customer’s request to speak to a representative between 
14 
the hours of 8:00 PM and 4:30 PM Monday through Friday (excluding 
15 
holidays).  However, the Company has historically calculated this metric 
16 
as: the sum of the system-wide number of calls answered by a 
17 
representative within 30 seconds (including callbacks) divided by total 
18 
number of calls answered (including callbacks).   
19 
3046


--- Page 78 ---
Case 23-E-0418; Case 23-G-0419 
 
REBUTTAL TESTIMONY OF THE CUSTOMER EXPERIENCE 
PANEL 
20

1 
methodology? 
2

3

4

5

6 
Metrics (“CSPI Order”) in Case 15-M-0566.

11 
10 basis points in 2020, 2021, 2022, respectively.

14 
should be included within the numerator and denominator of the

16

18

20

21 
for a customer service representative.  This is further detailed in the 
22 
3047

1 
Callbacks also allow representatives to be better prepared for the 
2 
conversation by reviewing the customer's information and context before 
3 
making the return call.  Offering a callback option can reduce the number 
4 
of abandoned calls, as customers who might otherwise hang up due to 
5 
long wait times can opt for a callback instead.  Including callbacks within 
6 
the Percent of Calls Answered within 30 Seconds metric ensures that the

Q. 
Why does the Panel believe that an exclusion is necessary if the 
1 
Company must rely on estimated usage because the customer and/or 
2 
other person who controls access to the meter  refuses to provide access? 
3 
A. 
If the Company cannot access the meter the resulting estimated bill 
4 
should be excluded.  Access to Company-owned metering equipment can 
5 
be refused outright by the customer, locks changed, and new keys not 
6 
provided to the utility.  Property gates surrounding buildings and properties 
7 
as well as access roads are found locked, and keys are not provided upon 
8 
request by the utility.  Meters can be blocked in basements or outside by a 
9 
multitude of items such as building materials, furniture, trash, etc.  Meters 
10 
are also rendered inaccessible because of guard dogs that are not 
11 
controlled or restrained by homeowners.  These issues are outside of the 
12 
control of the Company and should not result in a negative financial 
13 
impact on the Company.   
14 
Q. 
What is the additional exclusion the Panel is proposing? 
15 
A.

16 
outside of the Company’s control should also be excluded from the 
17 
measurement of this metric.  Abnormal operating conditions are deemed 
18 
to occur during any period of emergency, catastrophe, strike, natural 
19 
disaster, major storm, or other unusual event not in the Company’s control 
20 
affecting more than 10% of the customers in an operating area during any 
21 
month.  A major storm will have the same definition as set forth in 16 
22 
3053

NYCRR Part 97.  These conditions are likely to interrupt normal meter 
1

2 
will result in a greater number of estimated bills as meter readers assist to 
3 
restore service to customers.

E. 
NEGATIVE REVENUE ADJUSTMENTS 
6 
Q. 
Does SCSP propose any significant changes to the number of basis 
7 
points associated with NRAs? 
8 
A. 
Yes.  SCSP proposes four significant changes.  First, SCSP proposes 
9 
increasing the maximum NRA basis points associated with the Company’s 
10 
current CSPI metrics from 42 bps to 54 bps.  Second, SCSP proposes a 
11 
new Estimated Bill metric with an incremental maximum NRA of 18 bps.  
12 
Third, SCSP proposes that NRA values triple if the targets for two CSPI 
13 
metrics are missed in one year, which would result in a maximum NRA for 
14 
each metric of 54 bps and a maximum NRA for all four SCSP proposed 
15 
metrics of 216 bps, an increase of 414% from the current level of 42 bps.  
16 
Finally, SCSP proposes that if the Company misses any of the CSPI 
17 
metric targets in one year and then misses any of the CSPI metric targets 
18 
the following year, the maximum NRA for each metric would quadruple 
19 
producing a maximum NRA for each metric of 72 bps and a maximum 
20 
NRA for all four metrics of 288 bps, an increase of 586% from the current 
21 
level. 
22 
3054


--- Page 86 ---
Case 23-E-0418; Case 23-G-0419 
 
REBUTTAL TESTIMONY OF THE CUSTOMER EXPERIENCE 
PANEL 
28 
 
Q. 
Is the Panel  concerned with the level of NRAs that SCSP is proposing? 
1 
A. 
Yes, the Company has significant concerns with the level of NRAs 
2 
proposed by SCSP.  First, the Company believes that this potential 
3 
maximum NRA level is excessive and overly punitive.  Second, as 
4 
discussed further within the rebuttal testimonies of the Company’s Finance 
5 
Panel and Company Witness Nowak, the proposed NRA levels result in 
6 
significant financial uncertainty for the Company and its debt and equity 
7 
investors, which will lead to higher financing costs for customers.  Third, 
8 
the CSPI metrics are strongly correlated with one another, and it is 
9 
inappropriate to triple the NRA level for failure of more than one metric in 
10 
the same year given the relationship between the metrics.  Finally, the 
11 
CSPI metrics are strongly correlated with commodity price fluctuations and 
12 
an NRA should not be assessed due to market conditions beyond the 
13 
Company’s control. 
14 
Q.

15 
another. 
16 
A.

17

18 
CSPI metrics and electric and gas supply prices for the period 2018 
19

22

9

10

11 
mid-2023 in light of the COVID-19 Pandemic, the Parker-Mosley Act of 
12 
2020, the Parker-Richardson Act of 2021, and collection limitations 
13 
included within the Arrears Relief Programs.  While these influences were 
14 
impactful, other stakeholders (see Exhibit __ (CEP-1R) at pages 5-6) were 
15 
also communicating to the Company’s customers and impacting  the loss 
16 
of customer trust associated with the SAP-CIS transition.  Additionally, the 
17 
Company has not yet automated collections processes within the SAP-
18 
CIS and until that step is complete, it must rely on manual collections 
19 
efforts.  All of these factors have impacted customer arrears growth; 
20 
however, regardless of the impact of any one driver it is imperative that 
21 
the Company begin collections efforts.

Q. 
Please describe the results of manual collections effort for non-residential 
1 
customers. 
2 
A. 
As of November 30, 2023, 10,638 non-residential customers were in 
3 
arrears with a total arrears balance of $30.6 million.  Through November 
4 
30, 2023, 997 FTNs have been issued for Manual Non-Residential 
5 
Collections for a potential recovery of $12.5 million.  One hundred and 
6 
fifty-seven customers have paid in full, and 483 customers have entered 
7 
Deferred Payment Agreements (“DPAs”).  The average term for DPAs is 7 
8 
months.  Thirty-nine shutoffs have been completed to date, and the 
9 
majority of these customers remain locked as many of the commercial 
10 
premises were vacant.  Nearly $5.1 million has been collected through full 
11 
payments and DPA down payments. 
12

13

15 
1. A dedicated phone line staffed with an appropriately sized dedicated 
16

17 
the program. 
18 
2. Having multiple points of contact with the customer helped prevent 
19 
customer escalations, helped prevent confusion about the balance 
20 
owed, and helped prevent service interruptions.

4 
4. This program has also proven that non-residential businesses in the

15

16

17

18

19

20 
Q.

21 
3081


--- Page 113 ---
Case 23-E-0418; Case 23-G-0419 
 
REBUTTAL TESTIMONY OF THE CUSTOMER EXPERIENCE 
PANEL 
55 
 
A.

1 
the Company’s threshold for protection from service termination from 93 to 
2 
90 degrees or 85 degrees within more densely populated areas.  In 
3 
addition, the Company’s existing temperature threshold also factors in the 
4 
heat index, so it is highly likely that terminations will be suspended during 
5 
heat events where the surface temperature is below 93 degrees.  Existing 
6 
protections represent a balanced approach to collection which protects 
7 
vulnerable customers but mitigates uncollectible expense burdens for all 
8 
customers.

the scope of the project and conflicts with the SAP-CIS that would not be 
21 
resolved prior to the proposed launch target date.  This means that the 
22

costs of the project were not included in the Company’s Net Plant and

4

Q. 
SCSP recommends that the Commission require the Company to modify 
6 
its current method of tracking created jobs and retained jobs for every 
7 
approved application.  Does the Panel agree with this recommendation? 
8 
A. 
No.  Tracking created jobs or retained jobs beyond what the Company 
9 
currently does would be a notable incremental addition of work scope with 
10 
little to no added benefit. 
11 
Q. 
Please explain the Company’s current process for job tracking and any 
12 
benefit or challenges to modifying its program. 
13 
A. 
The Company’s economic development programs do not require or call for 
14 
the ongoing tracking of created or retained jobs, beyond taking a snapshot 
15 
of employment levels when an application is filed or a project initiated 
16 
through the presentation of the applicant’s NYS-45 that details employees 
17 
at a given time, and then comparing that NYS-45 to the figure after the 
18 
applicant meets its hiring objective and requests disbursement of funds.  
19 
Currently, economic development program requirements call for "a 
20 
minimum of 20 full time equivalent jobs must be retained or created."  This 
21 
requires Company personnel to review an economic development 
22 
3091


--- Page 123 ---
Case 23-E-0418; Case 23-G-0419

65 
 
application and a subsequent review of required documents that 
1 
demonstrate the hiring requirement has been accomplished by the 
2 
applicant.  Once the employment requirement, along with other 
3 
requirements is met, then funding can be released to the applicant.  
4 
Additionally, Central Hudson requires applicants to have participation from 
5 
Empire State Development Corporation (“ESD”), a local industrial 
6 
development agency, a community college, or a local development 
7 
corporation.  If any application does not contain participation from the 
8 
above-mentioned entities, Central Hudson may consider a letter of support 
9 
from the county of origin for such projects on a case-by-case basis.  ESD 
10 
and/or other governmental bodies have the expertise and capability to 
11 
monitor performance and adherence by applicants to any state or county 
12 
required employment commitments.  Therefore, tracking created jobs or 
13 
retained jobs beyond what the Company currently does would be a 
14 
notable incremental addition of work scope with little to no added benefit.
